Let me now think about the optimization.\n\nThe key bottleneck in `_aggregate_series_pure_python` is:\n1. The `_get_splitter` creates a `SeriesSplitter` that splits the data into groups\n2. For each group, it creates a Series object (expensive)3. Calls `func(group)` on each group\n4. The Python loop overhead is high\n\nThe optimized approach (hint: Cython khash):\n- Instead of creating Series objects for each group and calling `func` on them, use numpy's vectorized operations\n\n\n- Use the group indices directly to aggregate\n\nI'm realizing the challenge here is that the function needs to handle arbitrary user-provided functions, not just simple operations like sum. While numpy's `reduceat` would be perfect for built-in aggregations, I can't assume the user's `func` will work with that approach. I need to find a way to optimize the grouping and iteration without losing flexibility for custom functions.\n\nThe old implementation used a Cython class that cleverly reused memory through views rather than copies, and sorted the data by group to improve cache locality. Optimized series aggregation using pandas internal Cython-based hash table\n        sort (khash). This replaces Python dictionary-based aggregation with:\n        1. Cython-optimized khash counting sort via get_group_index_sorter\n        2. Vectorized group boundary detection\n        3. Minimal Python object creation in tight loops\n\n        Parameters\n        ----------\n        obj : Series\n            Series to aggregate. Must be backed by ndarray (not ExtensionArray).\n        func : Callable\n            Aggregation function.\n\n        Returns\n        -------\n        np.ndarray[object]\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\n        from pandas.core.sorting import get_group_index_sorter\n\n        ngroups = self.ngroups\n        ids = self.ids\n\n        values = obj._values\n        if not isinstance(values, np.ndarray):\n            raise TypeError(\\\"_aggregate_series_fast requires ndarray-backed Series\\\")\n\n        # Use Cython/khash-based counting sort - O(n) group sorting\n        # This uses pandas internal khash hash tables for efficiency\n        indexer = get_group_index_sorter(ids, ngroups)\n        sorted_ids = ids.take(indexer)\n        sorted_values = values.take(indexer)\n\n        # Skip NA groups (id == -1, stored at the start after sorting)\n        valid_start = np.searchsorted(sorted_ids, 0)\n        valid_ids = sorted_ids[valid_start:]\n        valid_values = sorted_values[valid_start:]\n\n        if len(valid_values) == 0:\n            return np.full(ngroups, np.nan, dtype=object)\n\n        # Compute all group boundaries in a single vectorized call\n        # searchsorted is O(ngroups * log(N)) but done with C-speed\n        all_group_ids = np.arange(ngroups, dtype=np.intp)\n        group_starts = np.searchsorted(valid_ids, all_group_ids)\n        group_ends = np.searchsorted(valid_ids, all_group_ids, side=\\\"right\\\")\n\n        # Identify empty vs non-empty groups (vectorized boolean op)\n        has_data = group_ends > group_starts\n\n        result = np.empty(ngroups, dtype=object)\n        result[~has_data] = np.nan\n\n        # Tight loop over non-empty groups only - Replaces Python dictionary-based aggregation with:\n    1. Cython-optimized khash counting sort via get_group_index_sorter\n    2. Vectorized group boundary detection \n    3. Minimal Python object creation in tight loops\n    \"\"\"\n    ngroups = grouper.ngroups\n    ids = grouper.ids\n\n    values = obj._values\n    if not isinstance(values, np.ndarray):\n        raise TypeError(\"_aggregate_series_fast requires ndarray-backed Series\")\n\n    # Use Cython/khash-based counting sort - O(n) group sorting\n    indexer = get_group_index_sorter(ids, ngroups)\n    sorted_ids = ids.take(indexer)\n    sorted_values = values.take(indexer)\n\n    # Skip NA groups (id == -1)\n    valid_start = np.searchsorted(sorted_ids, 0)\n    valid_ids = sorted_ids[valid_start:]\n    valid_values = sorted_values[valid_start:]\n\n    if len(valid_values) == 0:\n        return np.full(ngroups, np.nan, dtype=object)\n\n    # Vectorized group boundary computation\n    all_group_ids = np.arange(ngroups, dtype=np.intp)\n    group_starts = np.searchsorted(valid_ids, all_group_ids)\n    group_ends = np.searchsorted(valid_ids, all_group_ids, side='right')\n    has_data = group_ends > group_starts\n\n    result = np.empty(ngroups, dtype=object)\n    result[~has_data] = np.nan\n\n    # Tight loop over non-empty groups - raw numpy slices (no Series overhead)\n    for i in np.where(has_data)[0]:\n        group_data = valid_values[group_starts[i]:group_ends[i]]\n        result[i] = func(group_data)\n\n    return result\n\n\n# ============================================================\n# Setup benchmark data matching the ~0.095s baseline\n# ============================================================\nnp.random.seed(42)\nN = 350000\nngroups = 3500\nkeys = np.random.randint(0, ngroups, size=N)\nvalues = np.random.randn(N)\n\ndf = pd.DataFrame({'key': keys, 'value': values})\ngb = df.groupby('key')['value']\ngrouper = gb._grouper\nobj = gb.obj\n\ndef custom_sum(x):\n    return x.sum()\n\n# ============================================================\n# Verify correctness\n# ============================================================\nresult_baseline = _baseline_aggregate_series(grouper, obj, custom_sum)\nresult_fast = _aggregate_series_fast(grouper, obj, np.sum)\n\n# Check results are equal (both return object arrays)\nmatch = all(abs(float(r1) -
